16 
Q. 
Please provide an overall view of the testimony of Staff and intervenors concerning 
17 
the issues addressed in the Panel’s direct testimony. 
18 
A. 
The Panel’s direct testimony provided support for a series of targeted proposals intended 
19 
to both enhance the Companies’ customer service and assist the Commission and the State 
20 
in attaining their policy goals with respect to services provided to LMI customers and 
21 
Disadvantaged Communities, energy efficiency, and demand response.  The Companies’ 
22 


--- Page 7 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 5 of 60 
 
proposals included recommendations for incremental resources and programs, 
1 
modifications to the manner in which the Companies’ call centers are staffed and customers 
2 
pay their bills, and the management of uncollectible expenses in the wake of the COVID-
3 
19 pandemic. 
4 
 
5 
While Staff and the intervenors have accepted a few of these proposals in whole or in part, 
6 
these parties recommend rejection of many of them.  Moreover, Staff and certain 
7 
intervenors recommend a substantial increase in the Companies’ exposure to Negative 
8 
Revenue Adjustments (“NRAs”) for a failure to meet threshold metrics under proposed 
9 
revised Customer Service Performance Indicators (“CSPIs”) even though the record 
10 
establishes that the Companies have consistently met their customer performance targets 
11 
dating back to 2015.  As discussed more fully below, the recommended increase in NRA 
12 
exposure is not justified by any evidence whatsoever that the Companies need a greater 
13 
incentive to provide consistently high-quality customer service.  Moreover, the 
14 
recommended rejection of many of the customer proposals is unreasonable and at odds 
15 
with the Commission’s policy goals. 
16 
  
17

III. 
Customer Service Quality Program/Customer Service Performance Indicators 
1 
Q. 
The SCSP (at 48-77) provides a number of proposals concerning what the Panel refers 
2 
to in direct testimony as the Customer Service Quality Program, please confirm the 
3 
common terminology that you will use in addressing these issues. 
4 
A. 
Staff recommends that the Companies use the term “Customer Service Performance 
5 
Indicators” (“CSPIs”) to refer to what the Panel and UIU Witness Collar refer to as the 
6 
Customer Service Quality Program.  The Panel confirms that both terms refer to the same 
7 
mechanism, and consistent with the SCSP’s recommendation (at 49), the Panel will 
8 
henceforth refer to CSPIs. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
What are the current negative revenue adjustments (“NRAs”) that the Companies 
11 
are at risk for should their performance with respect to the CSPI metrics fall below 
12 
the established thresholds?  
13 
A. 
Under the current mechanism, KEDNY and KEDLI are at-risk for a total of $11.7 million 
14 
and $9.9 million, respectively, in NRAs if they fail to meet the established performance 
15 
thresholds. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
How do the SCSP and UIU Witness Collar propose to revise the NRAs that would 
18 
apply under the CSPI? 
19 
A. 
The SCSP (at 71-76) and Mr. Collar (at 13) propose to use 15 basis points for each of the 
20 
four CSPI metrics – a total of 60 basis points for the CSPI, which results in an increase in 
21 


--- Page 9 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 7 of 60 
 
annual NRA exposure to approximately $29.559 million for KEDNY and $18.971 million 
1 
for KEDLI. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Does the Panel support any of the recommendations to change the NRAs? 
4 
A. 
No, for several reasons.  First, as the SCSP (at 54-55) acknowledges, the Companies have 
5 
consistently exceeded all their CSPI performance thresholds since 2015.  Moreover, the 
6 
Companies’ CSPI mechanism was examined and approved just over two years ago by the 
7 
Commission in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases.  While the SCSP asserts (at 48) 
8 
that a CSPI is needed to “establish an incentive for each company to provide satisfactory 
9 
levels of customer service performance,” it offers no evidence that the current CSPI 
10 
mechanism has not provided the Companies with an appropriate incentive.  In fact, as part 
11 
of its annual audit, the 2022 Customer Service Performance Report, Staff found, “[f]or the 
12 
utilities that satisfactorily met their 2022 customer service performance metrics [including 
13 
KEDNY and KEDLI], the Commission’s performance measures serve as a strong incentive 
14 
to maintain performance and encourage the utilities to put significant efforts into 
15 
maintaining customer service.” (Case 23-M-0040 at 24). 
16 
 
 
 
17 
The SCSP and UIU Witness Collar are proposing to increase KEDNY and KEDLI’s 
18 
exposure to CSPI-related NRAs by 164 percent and 96 percent, respectively, without 
19 
providing any evidence that a greater incentive is necessary to encourage the Companies 
20 
to maintain adequate customer service.  These recommendations are contrary to both 
21 
common sense and applicable Commission precedent.  Specifically, in Case 16-G-0257, 
22 


--- Page 10 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 8 of 60 
 
the Commission found that where National Fuel Gas Distribution Corporation’s (“National 
1 
Fuel”) past customer service and gas safety performance was acceptable, the Commission 
2 
would not impose a CSPI mechanism in a one-year rate case.  In this case, unlike Case 16-
3 
G-0257, the Companies are not contending that no CSPI should be imposed.  Rather, the 
4 
Companies are contending that given the Companies past customer service performance, 
5 
there is no reason for the Commission to arbitrarily modify an existing CSPI to impose 
6 
substantially greater financial penalties as well as, in certain instances, more challenging 
7 
customer service targets that will only increase the likelihood that the Companies will incur 
8 
NRAs. 
9 
 
10 
Q. 
Is the Panel opposed to the SCSP and UIU Witness Collar’s proposals to convert the 
11 
dollar values to basis points? 
12 
A. 
The Companies do not object to the conversion of dollar values to basis points for the 
13 
purpose of determining NRAs.  However, the amount of basis points at-risk should be 
14 
equivalent to the dollar levels that are currently at-risk.  Based on the Companies’ filings 
15 
in these proceedings, KEDNY’s maximum NRA exposure of $11.7 million would be 
16 
converted to a maximum NRA of 24 basis points, while KEDLI’s maximum NRA 
17 
exposure of $9.9 million would be converted to a maximum NRA exposure of 31 basis 
18 
points.  
19 


--- Page 11 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 9 of 60 
 
 
1 
Q. 
In addition to proposing to increase the Companies’ NRA exposure under the CSPI, 
2 
the SCSP also proposes (at 63, 69) to increase the thresholds at which the Companies 
3 
would incur NRAs for the Customer Satisfaction Survey and, for KEDLI, the 
4 
Adjusted Bills metric.  Do you agree with these recommendations? 
5 
A. 
No, the SCSP’s recommendation concerning the Customer Satisfaction Survey is based on 
6 
consideration of five years’ worth of data between 2018-2022.  This period includes a 
7 
substantial period (March 2020 through September 2021 for KEDLI and March 2020 
8 
through November 2021 for KEDNY) during which the Companies implemented COVID-
9 
19-related collection moratoria.  During this period, the Companies attained unprecedented 
10 
levels of customer satisfaction (93.2 percent in 2020 and 92.0 percent in 2021 for KEDNY; 
11 
92.2 percent in 2020 and 92.0 percent in KEDLI).  The Companies do not expect that these 
12 
levels are representative of the levels of customer satisfaction that can be attained in a 
13 
period in which the Companies are enforcing collections.  Thus, the Panel submits that 
14 
customer satisfaction data for this period should not be used to set minimum customer 
15 
satisfaction thresholds for the purpose of assessing NRAs.  The Companies’ existing 
16 
Customer Satisfaction thresholds should remain in place. 
17 
 
 
 
18 
With respect to the Adjusted Bills minimum threshold, the SCSP’s recommendation that 
19 
KEDLI's existing target should be reduced from 1.5 percent to 0.62 percent is based solely 
20 
on the fact that KEDLI’s actual performance has exceeded this threshold for the past five 
21 
years.  From the Companies’ perspective, ratcheting up performance measures based solely 
22 


--- Page 12 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 10 of 60 
 
on past good performance is unreasonable and contrary to sound regulatory policy.  The 
1 
SCSP’s recommendation effectively amounts to a proposal to punish the Companies for 
2 
past good performance and would provide a disincentive for the Companies to achieve 
3 
better levels of performance in the future.  For these reasons, the SCSP’s recommendation 
4 
to impose a more stringent CSPI threshold for Adjusted Bills for KEDLI should be rejected. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
UIU Witness Collar recommends (at 8) that KEDLI’s existing thresholds for PSC 
7 
Complaints and Adjusted Bills should be modified.  Do you agree with those 
8 
recommendations? 
9 
A. 
No.  UIU Witness Collar’s recommendations are even more punitive than the SCSP’s 
10 
proposals.  Specifically, Mr. Collar would propose thresholds of 0.6 complaints per 
11 
100,000 customer and 0.20 percent for Adjusted Bills.  The existing thresholds for 
12 
customer complaints of 1.0 per 100,000 customers is already the most stringent in New 
13 
York.  UIU Witness Collar’s proposed thresholds would be far and away the lowest 
14 
thresholds in New York State for PSC Complaints and Adjusted Bills and would do nothing 
15 
more than punish KEDLI for having provided a high level of customer service during a 
16 
period that included the COVID-19 pandemic.  In the absence of any evidence that 
17 
KEDLI’s customer service has declined or is likely to decline in the future, there is no 
18 
reasonable justification for adopting the thresholds recommended by Mr. Collar. 
19 
 
20 


--- Page 13 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 11 of 60 
 
Q. 
The SCSP recommends (at 57-58) that the Companies CSPIs should be modified to 
1 
establish performance tiers for each metric.  Do you agree with this recommendation? 
2 
A. 
As we have stated previously, it is generally our understanding that the Commission will 
3 
not require significant changes to a CSPI in a one-year rate case where there is no evidence 
4 
that a utility’s customer service is likely to decline.  However, in the context of multi-year 
5 
settlement discussions, the Companies are willing to explore a tiered approach provided 
6 
that existing thresholds for the CSPI metric and the total value of the potential NRAs 
7 
remain unchanged. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Did the SCSP propose any additional recommendations regarding the Companies’ 
10 
CSPI reporting? 
11 
A. 
Yes, the SCSP (at 70-71) recommended the Companies provide disaggregated billing data 
12 
from 2019 through 2022 for purposes of assessing the Companies’ estimated billing 
13 
processes and procedures. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Do the Companies track the disaggregated data in the form requested by the SCSP? 
16 
A. 
No.  The Companies, as noted by the SCSP (at 70), file monthly CSPI reports with the 
17 
Commission.  The monthly reports include an estimated bill calculation by month for each 
18 
company and a year-to-date estimated bill average.  As set forth in the “Customer Service 
19 
Quality and Performance Indicator Reports – Procedure Document” provided to Staff as 
20 
Attachment 1 to IR No. DPS-420, included in Exhibit ___ (CP-1R), the Companies 
21 
calculate the estimated bill information using missed meter read data from the Field 
22 


--- Page 14 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 12 of 60 
 
Collection System (“FCS”).  The FCS does not disaggregate data in the form requested.  
1 
Nevertheless, the Companies are willing to work with Staff to identify data sets available 
2 
from the CRIS and CSS systems for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, that would help 
3 
with Staff’s review of the Companies’ billing processes and procedures. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
The SCSP (at 71) indicates its data request is tied to statewide concerns with the 
6 
frequency and accuracy of estimated bills, particularly estimated bills for multiple 
7 
consecutive months.  Do the Companies share Staff’s concern? 
8 
A. 
The Companies share a commitment to ensuring customers receive accurate bills. Limiting 
9 
the number of estimated bills is an important part of that effort.  To that end, the Companies 
10 
successfully completed Automated Meter Reading (“AMR”) initiatives, which have 
11 
reduced the number of estimated bills customers receive.  Specifically, KEDNY has seen 
12 
a significant reduction in estimated reads from highs approaching 320,000 estimates per 
13 
month as recently as 2016.  
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Despite these efforts, why is KEDNY’s estimated bill rate higher than KEDLI’s? 
16 
A. 
As noted by the SCSP (at 70), KEDLI’s estimated meter read rate from 2019 to 2022 was 
17 
well below one percent, whereas KEDNY’s rate over that same period rose to 6.49 percent. 
18 
The Companies believe there are several reasons contributing to the difference – most 
19 
notably the predominance of accessible outdoor meters in KEDLI’s service territory and 
20 
the newer age of the majority of KEDLI AMR Encoder Receiver Transmitters (“ERTs”).  
21 
As explained in the direct testimony of the Companies’ Gas Safety Panel (at 19-21), 
22 


--- Page 15 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 13 of 60 
 
KEDLI’s meter relocation program has successfully moved approximately 14,444 meters 
1 
outside.  For KEDNY, however, space constraints and New York City Department of 
2 
Buildings code interpretations have significantly impaired meter relocation efforts.  
3 
Likewise, a majority of the KEDNY ERTs, many of which are located indoors, are now 
4 
well beyond their 20-year useful life and beginning to fail at higher rates. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Was the impact of these operational differences exacerbated by the COVID-19 
7 
pandemic? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  For KEDNY, COVID-related restrictions and policies resulted in an increase in the 
9 
backlog of failed units due to the high percentage of aging indoor meters/ERTs. Following 
10 
Governor Cuomo’s emergency declaration on March 7, 2020 (16 NYCRR §8.202), the 
11 
Companies stopped having crews enter homes and business for non-essential services, 
12 
which included manually accessing meters/ERTs that were not transmitting AMR meter 
13 
reads.  To protect the general health, safety, and welfare of customers, the Commission 
14 
also authorized the Companies to postpone rate increases and waive certain fees for 
15 
collections-related activities and non-essential services, including meter-access charges.  
16 
(Order issued March 25, 2023 in Case 17-E-0238, et al.).  The Company effectuated the 
17 
meter-access fee waiver by disabling functionality on the AMR meters used to 
18 
automatically initiate estimated meter-read letters for customers and impose meter-access 
19 
fees.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 16 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 14 of 60 
 
 
Also, KEDNY would have installed or replaced ERTs as part of its normal operations, such 
1 
as responding to customer requests for non-essential services, meter replacements, or 
2 
inspections; with those activities on hold due to the pandemic-related work restrictions, the 
3 
backlog of non-communicating ERTs grew to a level that cannot be addressed with existing 
4 
resources. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
If current resources are insufficient to address the backlog of ERT failures, how does 
7 
KEDNY propose to address the situation and reduce the estimated meter reads? 
8 
A. 
As set forth in the direct and rebuttal testimony of the Gas Infrastructure Operations Panel 
9 
(“GIOP”), KEDNY proposed two initiatives to address the increase in ERT failures and 
10 
reduce the backlog: an AMR ERT Replacement Purchase Program and a Proactive ERT 
11 
Replacement Program. (KEDNY GIOP direct testimony at 59, Exhibit___(GIOP-5) at 17-
12 
19 and 180-182, and GIOP rebuttal testimony at 55).  Taken together, KEDNY expects 
13 
these programs will enable the company to address a significant portion of the non-
14 
communicating ERTs that accumulated during and after the COVID-related pause on non-
15 
essential activity.  KEDNY (GIOP at 59, 100) has further proposed to support this effort 
16 
with 14 incremental FTEs in the Rate Year, increasing to 23 incremental FTEs by Data 
17 
Year 3. 
18 
 
19 


--- Page 17 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 15 of 60 
 
Q. 
Do the Companies propose any additional outreach to further reduce the average 
1 
number of bills based on estimated meter reads? 
2 
A. 
Yes.  Customers can currently access the Companies’ website for information on how to 
3 
submit a meter read, including logging into their accounts to provide the information, 
4 
calling the Companies’ customer service number, or scheduling an appointment.  In 
5 
addition, the Companies are developing a proactive outreach campaign to inform 
6 
customers of these options, which will likely include an opportunity for customers to email 
7 
a picture of their meter to the company – a capability KEDLI successfully used 
8 
approximately 10 years ago when converting its customer service system and which is 
9 
currently being used by the Companies’ Massachusetts affiliate. 
10 
 
11 
Q.       Does the SCSP make any recommendations regarding the Companies’ tariffs? 
12 
A.        Yes.  The SCSP (at 100) recommends adding tariff language that expressly addresses 
13 
when customers must receive a bill for services rendered by the Companies and 
14 
establishing procedures to inform customers of irregularities that cause a billing delay. 
15 
 
16 
Q.       What is the Companies response to the SCSP’s proposed tariff language? 
17 
A.        The Companies are unaware of specific concerns regarding the regularity of the monthly 
18 
bills they issue.  Nevertheless, they are open to working with Staff to understand the 
19 
concerns and how to best resolve them.  Specific to the tariff language proposed, the 
20 
Companies would like to better understand the dates and requirements it would impose 
21 
relative to the Companies billing cycles and current procedures.  In addition, the tariff 
22 


--- Page 18 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 16 of 60 
 
proposal includes process changes that would require time to develop, as well as costs and 
1 
resources to implement (e.g., IT support for coding).  Those costs and resources are not 
2

V. 
Customer Experience Programs 
1 
Q. 
The SCSP (at 78-80) recommends that the Commission should reject the Companies’ 
2 
proposals to use vendor call center employees outside of New York and near offshore.  
3 
Do you agree with these recommendations? 
 
4 
A. 
No.  The Companies’ third-party vendors provide flexible work schedules with work-from-
5 
home options to attract and retain quality employees.  Even with this flexibility, attrition 
6 
rates remain high for stateside employees.  The Companies continue to compete with other 
7 
types of employment, which was not the Companies’ experience previously and which has 
8 
resulted in increased difficulty in attracting and retaining New York-based employees.  
9 
Enabling third-party vendors to use resources outside New York State will allow them to 
10 
attract candidates from a wider range of employment markets, affording them the 
11 
opportunity to achieve greater efficiencies and reduce attrition.  Under the Companies’ 
12 
proposal, employees of the third-party could be located outside of New York but within 
13 
the continental United States for all English calls and near offshore for Spanish calls.  The 
14 
experience of National Grid’s Massachusetts affiliate is that the current attrition rate for 
15 
Guatemala resources supporting Spanish-speaking call volume is at less than one percent 
16 
monthly. 
17 
 
18

VI. 
Uncollectibles 
8 
Q. 
What are the recommendations by the SRRP (at 54-57) and NYC Witness Chait (at 
9 
42-43) regarding the uncollectible rate?  
10 
A. 
Staff agrees with the Companies’ use of a three-year average of write-off percentages to 
11 
determine the uncollectible expense for both KEDLI and KEDNY utilizing the years 2017-
12 
2019.  However, the SRRP recommends an adjustment to the calculation for KEDNY to 
13 
reflect a decline in write-off percentages over the 2017-2019 three-year period for 
14 
KEDNY.  This adjustment would reduce KEDNY’s uncollectible expenses by $1.998 
15 
million.  NYC Witness Chait opposes using a three-year average and proposes using only 
16 
the 2019 write off rate to determine the uncollectible expense.  
17 
  
18 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with NYC Witness Chait’s recommendations?  
19 
A. 
The Companies disagree with Ms. Chait’s recommendation to rely solely on the 2019 
20 
write-off percentages.  The use of a three-year average to calculate the net write-off rate is 
21 
consistent with the methodology used in the 2019 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases and the 
22 


--- Page 30 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 28 of 60 
 
2017 and 2020 NMPC Rate Cases.  Moreover, the use of the three-year average reduces 
1 
the chance of an anomaly that could have taken place during one particular year.  A three-
2 
year average is a fair method of predicting future rates under the circumstances of these 
3 
cases.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with SRRP’s recommendation to use the three-year average 
6 
of the net decline in write-off rates during the 2017-2019 period to establish KEDNY’s 
7 
write-off rate? 
8 
A. 
No.  Adjusting the three-year average calculation for KEDNY would not be appropriate 
9 
because there are a number of factors that impact the net write-off rate, including changes 
10 
in commodity costs.  This is illustrated by the fact that while KEDNY’s write-off rate went 
11 
down over the 2017-2019 period, KEDNY’s uncollectible expense actually increased from 
12 
$14.6 million in 2017 to $16.3 million in 2018.  In seeking to impute an adjustment to 
13 
KEDNY’s write-off rate, the SRRP is doing nothing more than imposing an arbitrary 
14 
disallowance of uncollectible expense on KEDNY.  The methodology for determining 
15 
uncollectible expense should be consistent for both KEDNY and KEDLI, reflecting a three-
16 
year average of write-off percentages without adjustment. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Do KEDNY’s current circumstances with respect to customer arrearages support the 
19 
reduction in KEDNY’s write-off percentages recommended by the SCSP? 
20 
A. 
No.  As of July 2023, KEDNY has approximately 215,000 accounts that are in arrears for 
21 
more than 60 days.  Over the period 2017-2019, the average number of KEDNY accounts 
22 


--- Page 31 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 29 of 60 
 
that were in arrears was approximately 175,000.  Given the increase in the number of 
1 
accounts in arrears, KEDNY is unlikely to experience a reduction in its net write off 
2 
percentage in the Rate Year. 
3 
   
4 
Q. 
The SRRP asserts that it is reasonable to assume that KEDNY’s efforts toward 
5 
reducing uncollectible expenses support the imputation of a further reduction in 
6 
KEDNY’s write-off rate.  Do you agree?

A. 
No.  The SRRP (at 56) points to the fact that the SCSP is recommending an incremental

20 collection FTEs in support of its position.  This claim ignores the fact that the

Companies sought 30 incremental FTEs to restore their resources to pre-pandemic levels.  
10 
As discussed more fully below, based on the fact that the number of KEDNY accounts has 
11 
grown to approximately 215,000, KEDNY would now actually need 37 Field Collection 
12

18

Case 23-G-0225

Q.

A.

average number of accounts in arrearage in the 2017-2019 period, creating a likelihood that 
12 
write-offs will increase in the Rate Year.  Given this uncertainty, it is the Panel’s position 
13 
that the circumstances of this case are much like the situation in Consolidated Edison 
14 
Company of New York, Inc.’s (“Con Edison”) 2008 rate proceeding (Case 08-E-0539) in

which the Commission allowed a full reconciliation of uncollectible expense in a one-year

Field Collection Employees 
9 
Q.

A.

40,000 customers or 25 percent from the three-year average of 2017-2019 to July 2023.  
19 
As of July 2023, KEDNY has approximately 215,000 accounts in arrears which are 
20 
projected to result in the need for approximately 234,000 field visits and 38,000 
21 
terminations.  Based on a three-year average for the period 2017-2019 of field visits per 
22

20 
chill temperatures are below 32 degrees Fahrenheit.  PULP witness Yates (at 72-73) 
21

22

Case 23-G-0226

Page 33 of 60 
 
 
1 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with these recommendations?  
2 
A. 
Annually, the Companies have agreed to additional voluntary protections requested by the 
3 
Department of Public Service, local service agencies, and other groups regarding 
4 
terminations for non-payment during the winter, including: 
5 
• For the period from November 1 through April 15, the Companies will accept all 
6 
HEAP payments and provide the customer with continued or restored service 
7 
regardless of the amount due and/or the customer’s payment status.  
8 
• During the Cold Weather Period, the Companies will consider any Regular and/or 
9 
Emergency HEAP payment as entitling the applicant to a fair and reasonable DPA, 
10 
regardless of any previous DPA defaults.   
11 
• The Companies will refrain from scheduling residential service terminations on 
12 
days when the local weather forecast predicts below-freezing temperatures.   
13 
• The Companies will establish a voluntary moratorium on winter terminations for 
14 
customers with an EBD designation. 
15 
 
16 
The Companies believe there is no need for additional restrictions associated with the wind 
17 
chill adjusted temperature.  The Companies will continue to voluntarily agree to annual 
18 
cold weather protections, including refraining from scheduling residential service 
19 
terminations on days when the local weather forecast predicts below-freezing temperatures, 
20 
which is at 32 degrees Fahrenheit without wind chill.

IX. 
Residential Credit Card/Debit Card Fees 
1 
Q. 
The SCSP (at 91) opposes the Companies’ proposal to socialize and reconcile credit 
2 
card/debit card (“CC/DC”) fees in rates.  Do you agree with this recommendation?  
3 
A. 
The Companies disagree with the SCSP recommendation that the Commission reject the 
4 
use of the no-fee model to socialize residential CC/DC fees in rates.  While the SCSP cites 
5 
a concern that transaction fees are likely to increase, this is not consistent with the 
6 
Companies’ experience.  The Companies have utilized Speedpay as their third-party card 
7 
processor since 2009.  Initially, the fee for residential customers was $2.25 per transaction 
8 
until 2019 when it was reduced to $1.99 per transaction.  In 2022, it was further reduced to 
9 
$1.75, which illustrates the Companies’ commitment and ability to lower fees for our 
10 
customers.  The Companies have been able to negotiate lower fees for customers by 
11 
utilizing a request-for-proposals process to obtain lower fees from Speedpay.  The 
12 
Companies are also aware that, to the extent the Companies can increase the amount of 
13 
CC/DC payments, there is an opportunity to work with Speedpay to apply for the Visa 
14 
Utility Rate, which will further reduce the card processing fee.  In proposing to socialize 
15 
CC/DC fees, the Companies are attempting to satisfy customers that overwhelmingly agree 
16 
that they would use credit/debit cards to pay their bills if there was no fee.  In this regard, 
17 
the Companies conducted a survey in July 2023 that asked, “Which, if any, of the following 
18 
would keep you from using a Debit or Credit Card to pay your monthly National Grid bill?” 
19 
311 customers responded to the survey with 70 percent saying the fee prevents them from 
20 
using this electronic payment method.  Based on this response, the Companies submit that 
21 


--- Page 37 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 35 of 60 
 
a significant number of customers see the implementation of the no fee CC/DC model as 
1 
an element of higher quality customer service. 
2 
 
3 
The Companies also see a benefit to the no-fee model for low-income customers that want 
4 
to use a debit or credit card to pay their bill.  In recent months, approximately 13 percent 
5 
of credit/debit card payments were made by low-income customers.  The socialization of 
6 
these costs will aid low-income customers by reducing the cost to individual customers 
7 
that use this method to pay their utility bills.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
The SCSP asserts (at 91) that New York utilities that have adopted the no-fee model 
10 
have seen an increase in both aggregate fees and per transaction fee rates.  Do you 
11 
agree with this claim? 
12 
A. 
The Panel agrees that aggregate CC/DC fees have increased for New York utilities after 
13 
adoption of the no-fee model but disputes the claim that per-transaction fees have 
14 
increased.  Attached as Exhibit ___ (CP-2R) are the quarterly CC/DC payment reports filed 
15 
by Central Hudson in Case 20-E-0428, Orange & Rockland in Case 18-E-0067, and 
16 
NYSEG and RGE in Case 19-E-0378.  These reports show that: 
17 
1. Central Hudson’s per-transaction rate stayed consistent at $1.85 between October 
18 
2021 through June 2023; 
19 
2. Orange and Rockland’s per-transaction fee dropped from $2.20 in the first quarter 
20 
of 2019 to $2.10 in the fourth quarter of 2021; and 
21 


--- Page 38 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 36 of 60 
 
3. NYSEG and RGE’s per-transaction costs have dropped from $2.03 and $2.72, 
1 
respectively, in the second quarter of 2021 to $1.31 and $1.56, respectively, in the 
2 
second quarter of 2023. 
3 
 
4 
The Companies disagree with the SCSP that the fact that aggregate charges for CC/DC 
5 
fees increases for utilities provides a basis to reject the no fee model.  From the Companies’ 
6 
perspective, this increase in costs demonstrates that eliminating per transaction fees is seen 
7 
by customers as improving customer service.  More importantly, however, as discussed 
8 
above, the Companies believe that if they can implement the no-fee model for CC/DC fees, 
9 
they will be able to achieve further reductions in the per-transaction fees that would benefit 
10 
all customers. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
Both SCSP and NYC Witness Chait oppose the Companies’ proposal to reconcile the 
13 
total costs socializing CC/DC fees.  Do you agree? 
14 
A. 
No.  Given the uncertainty associated with the transaction fees and customer adoption, the 
15 
Companies believe that a full symmetrical reconciliation of these costs is reasonable.  A 
16 
number of other New York utilities currently reconcile CC/DC fees and adoption of the 
17 
Companies’ proposal would put them on the same footing as other utilities.   
18 
 
19

1

2

4

5

6 
with the Green Button Connect system. 
7 
 
8

10

11

12 
work, quality assurance, controls, and compliance, as well as program management and 
13 
delivery.  Further, the Clean Energy 2.0 support FTE is for both the existing InDemand 
14 
system as well as the future Clean Energy 2.0 system.  Previous data presented shows the 
15 
year-over-year increase in work requests required to keep the existing system running, 
16 
along with updates needed for existing and new program/product delivery.  Also, the

maintain that system.

8

9

10 
significant amount of time and effort not only to coordinate within National Grid regarding 
11 
the customers’ needs, but to help educate, motivate, and lease across multiple areas of these 
12 
customers’ own large organizations to put together longer-term plans that all parties can 
13

16 
field and their community will help to accelerate the uptake of developing technologies.

18 
existing Customer Solutions team to work with additional large customers that they are not 
19 
presently able to fully attend to.  All these efforts will be required as the Companies work 
20 
toward the changes driven by the NE:NY Interim Review recommendation for 2026 – 2030 
21 
and the required transition in 2024 and 2025.  These changes will require longer project

deemed “strategic” require more education, in depth review, analysis, and regular

interaction with the customer. 
3 
 
4 
Regarding the utilization of current energy efficiency FTEs, these FTEs are being utilized 
5 
in many areas of the full market which include working with the channel partners, 
6 
increasing the participation in the Demand Response programs, working on technical 
7 
reviews required for Commercial and Industrial weatherization, as well as program 
8 
management for new programs the Companies are trying to establish in the market.  This 
9 
level of staffing has enabled us to achieve just above target through calendar year 2022.  
10 
But the Companies forecast significant gaps in their ability to achieve the NE:NY targets 
11 
in calendar year 2023 and beyond.   Therefore, these FTEs are fully dedicated to the energy 
12 
efficiency efforts noted above and will not have the bandwidth required to deliver the 
13 
growth in account management activities that is undoubtedly required.

Demand Response Costs 
7 
Q.

8

9

10

11 
response O&M (“DROM”) component of the delivery rate adjustment, but she opposes the 
12

13 
and Bring Your Own Thermostat (“BYOT”) programs via the DROM, and instead supports 
14 
recovery of those costs via base delivery rates. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Do the Companies agree with NYC Witness Chait’s recommendation? 
17 
A. 
No.  It is standard practice among utilities in New York to recover all non-labor program 
18 
costs, including marketing and administrative, through a surcharge.  For example, this is 
19 
the practice approved for Niagara Mohawk’s gas and electric demand response programs 
20 
and the practice utilized for Con Edison’s electric demand response programs.  It is also 
21 
worthy of note that the marketing and administrative costs associated with the gas demand 
22 


--- Page 52 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 50 of 60 
 
response programs are relatively small in comparison to total program expenditures (they 
1 
represented approximately nine percent of the winter 2022-23 DR program costs) and that 
2

3 
vary based on the level of participation in that program.  From the Companies’ perspective, 
4 
these factors support continued recovery of these demand response costs through a 
5 
surcharge.

11

XIII. DPAs and EBD Customers 
17 
Q. 
What were the concerns raised by PULP Witness Yates regarding DPAs?  
18 
A. 
PULP Witness Yates proposes (at 66) that KEDNY adopt a procedure to take financial 
19 
information for non-standard DPAs over the phone.  Also, Mr. Yates proposes (at 67) that 
20 
the Companies adopt a goal of 33 percent DPAs compared to accounts in arrears. 
21 
 
22 


--- Page 53 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 51 of 60 
 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s response to these proposals?  
1 
A. 
KEDNY customers currently have an option to do a Financial Statement and provide proof 
2 
without going into a walk-in office.  The customer can complete the Online Financial 
3 
Statement and upload proof through the form.  There are constraints in the CRIS customer 
4 
system, which is used for KEDNY customers, that limit the input of financial statement 
5 
information directly into the customer system.  Once the conversion of KEDNY accounts 
6 
from the legacy CRIS system into the CSS system is complete, KEDNY customers will 
7 
have the option to complete a Financial Statement over the phone in a similar fashion that 
8 
Niagara Mohawk and KEDLI customers have today.  The Companies submit that once the 
9 
conversion takes place, the issue raised by PULP will be resolved. 
10 
 
11 
Regarding the establishment of a goal of a participation rate of 33 percent of customers in 
12 
arrears enrolled in a DPA, there are several factors that influence the participation rate of 
13 
in-arrears customers in the Companies’ DPA programs. The Companies do not have direct 
14 
influence over many of these factors, and thus cannot be expected to be able to directly 
15 
manage DPA participation rates. 
16 
 
17 
One factor is the perceived risk of service termination. For example, during COVID-19 
18 
moratorium on service terminations, the number of accounts enrolled in an active DPA fell 
19 
by about 50 percent across the Companies, which was outside of the Companies’ control. 
20 
In addition, requirements regarding service terminations during the winter may impact 
21 
whether a customer enrolls in a DPA, which is also outside the Companies’ control. 
22 


--- Page 54 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 52 of 60 
 
In addition, customers who default from a DPA are not eligible for another DPA unless 
1 
their financial circumstances have significantly changed, in accordance with HEFPA 
2 
regulations. PULP Witness Yates’ recommended metric divides the number of active DPA 
3 
participants by the number of accounts in arrears. Because not all accounts in this proposed 
4 
denominator are actually eligible for a DPA, it is an inappropriate metric. 
5 
 
6 
Further, KEDNY and KEDLI both offer alternative short-term payment products that 
7 
customers may prefer over a DPA.  These short-term credit products are most appropriate 
8 
for accounts that are only 30-60 days in arrears.  This aging bracket is the largest 30-day 
9 
aging bracket amount of all accounts in arrears.  These short-term credit products are not 
10 
included in payment agreement counts, which may be a different practice than other 
11 
utilities.  Therefore, if the customers in these short-term credit products were included in 
12 
the payment agreement count, then the payment agreement rate would be higher.  For the 
13 
foregoing reasons, PULP Witness Yates’ recommendation concerning a DPA participation 
14 
rate goal should be rejected. 
15

of four proposed EAMs providing a maximum of 14 basis points in potential earnings to

continue to evaluate and develop future EAM opportunities to include in the NE:NY 
1

2 
 
3 
Q.  
Please address the SEP’s testimony that the proposed Demand Response EAM metric 
4 
aimed at maximizing participation in demand response programs does not run 
5 
counter, but instead, aligns with the Companies’ business model. 
6 
A. 
The Companies disagree that the Demand Response EAM would reward the Companies 
7 
for performance that already aligns with their business model.  Although Staff is correct 
8 
that the Companies currently include the Demand Response programs in their design day 
9 
forecast to meet design day supply needs (SEP at 78), the use of gas demand response is 
10 
not “business as usual” as no other New York gas utility is operating as extensive a gas 
11 
demand response program to reduce peak demand.  The Demand Response EAM targets 
12 
have been established to incentivize the Companies to increase participation in the demand 
13 
response program even more aggressively which not only will meet customer peak day 
14 
supply needs but also potentially avoid the need for new gas infrastructure.  Consequently, 
15 
demand response is in essence a form of Non-Pipe Alternatives (“NPAs”), although not 
16 
targeting a specific infrastructure project.  The reduction of traditional plant-in-service is 
17 
something the Commission in its Gas Planning Order1 has recognized can afford 
18 
shareholders the opportunity to share in the net benefits, because the traditional utility 
19 
business model would be to pursue new gas infrastructure to meet customer demand.  
20 
 
1 Case 20-G-0131, Proceeding on the Motion of the Commission in Regard to Gas Planning Procedures 
(“Gas Planning Proceeding”), Order Adopting Gas System Planning Process (issued and effective May 12, 
2022) (“Gas Planning Order”) at p. 43. 


--- Page 58 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 56 of 60 
 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with the SEP’s recommendation to reject the Demand Response 
1 
EAM? 
2 
A. 
No.  The DR EAM will incentivize the Companies not only to increase enrollment in the 
3 
demand response programs from the enrollment achieved in the first three years of this 
4 
innovative portfolio of programs, but also to innovate further to improve performance (and 
5 
thereby further reduce peak gas demand) during events.  Moreover, it will assist in reducing 
6 
the need for future traditional gas plant in-service.  For these reasons, the EAM targets will 
7 
require the Companies to perform in a way that does not align with its business model and 
8 
is not “business as usual” in order to meet the established targets.  One of the main policy 
9 
directions in the Commission’s REV Track Two Order ( Order Adopting a Ratemaking 
10 
and Utility Revenue Model Policy Framework  “Proceeding on Motion of the Commission 
11 
in Regard to Reforming the Energy Vision” (Case 14-M-0101), Issued and effective May 
12 
19, 2016) is that cost-of-service ratemaking, while it will remain applicable to conventional 
13 
utility investments for the near future, inhibits innovation in general, and discourages 
14 
numerous activities that utilities need to undertake to implement REV.  Consumers benefit 
15 
when utilities aggressively pursue more economic alternatives to traditional rate based 
16 
capital investment.” (REV Track Two Order at 23).  This Demand Response EAM furthers 
17 
this REV Track Two policy.  
18 
 
19 


--- Page 59 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 57 of 60 
 
Q. 
Have other utilities in New York state received approval of EAMs that reward 
1 
achievement on their demand response programs? 
2 
A. 
Yes, in July of this year the Commission issued an order adopting the Con Edison joint 
3 
proposal (Cases 22-E-0064 and 22-G-0065), in which it approved an Electric Demand 
4 
Response EAM that “promotes grid flexibility by developing a larger and more reliable 
5 
demand response resource that can be called on to reduce peak demand … during system 
6 
contingencies.”  (Appendix 8, p. 8).  Although that EAM and the Gas Demand Response 
7 
EAM address different energy types, and although there are some differences in the 
8 
methodologies used to calculate the respective EAMs, the principle of promoting system 
9 
flexibility and reducing peak demand by a method that is not business as usual is the same.  
10 
There is no reason why demand response should be viewed as behavior that can provide 
11 
customer benefits and can be incentivized for electric utilities but not for gas utilities. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Has the Commission approved other incentive mechanisms for programs or solutions 
14 
that reduce peak gas demand? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has in principle endorsed an incentive mechanism for NPAs for 
16 
both the Companies and other utilities in the state.  That mechanism is intended to 
17 
incentivize utilities to pursue demand-side solutions in lieu of infrastructure projects on 
18 
which they would earn a traditional rate of return.  Although NPAs are intended to address 
19 
localized areas, whereas the gas demand response programs address systemwide peaks, the 
20 
principle behind both are the same - they reduce peak gas demand, thereby deferring or 
21 
avoiding the need for new or upgraded gas infrastructure and cause the Companies to forgo 
22 


--- Page 60 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 58 of 60 
 
a rate of return on that deferred or avoided infrastructure investment.  Because they both 
1 
serve the same purpose, if NPAs are granted such an incentive mechanism, gas demand 
2 
response should be as well. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please address the SEP’s claim (at 82-83) that the EAP enrollment EAM metric does 
5 
not match the criteria the Commission provided for customer engagement EAMs in 
6 
the REV Track Two Order. 
7 
A. 
The Companies disagree that the customer engagement referenced in the REV Track Two 
8 
Order applies to the EAP enrollment EAM. In the REV Track Two Order the Commission 
9 
was referencing engagement by customers in certain programs such as DER where the 
10 
utility can play a facilitating role by connecting customers to vendors, but where the market 
11 
participants also play the greater role.  In contrast, the Companies’ proposed EAP 
12 
Enrollment EAM is intended to encourage further investment and innovation to reach 
13 
customers in Disadvantage Communities who can benefit from the EAP program and 
14 
become eligible for benefits and savings where the Companies play the major role.  An 
15 
EAP enrollment EAM will advance the CLCPA’s goal of affordability as a key value in 
16 
the energy transition. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Please address the SEP’s claim (at 83) that the EAP enrollment EAM metric would 
19 
reward Companies with a windfall in the event of an economic downturn. 
20 
A. 
The Companies disagree that the EAP enrollment metric would reward the Companies with 
21 
a windfall during an economic downturn because the metric can be designed to be adjusted 
22 


--- Page 61 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 59 of 60 
 
to account for an increase in the number of customers eligible for the EAP program due to 
1 
economic conditions.  The Company proposes to work with Staff and the parties to develop 
2 
a mutually agreeable metric and targets that account for economic downturns. 
3 
 
4 
Q.  
Does the Panel agree with the SEP’s recommendation not to approve the EAP 
5 
Enrollment EAM? 
6 
A. 
No.  The EAP Enrollment EAM would incentivize the Companies to further innovate to 
7 
increase awareness of the available benefits to those in Disadvantaged Communities, goes 
8 
beyond business as usual, and advances the CLCPA’s goals of an equitable and affordable 
9 
energy transition.  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Why is it important for the Commission to continue EAMs for utilities? 
12 
A. 
In the REV Track Two Order the Commission established a new set of incentives it termed 
13 
EAMs that were geared towards near term measures “to create customer savings and 
14 
develop market-enabling tools.”  (REV Track Two Order at 12).  In their direct testimony, 
15 
the SEP states (at 57) that the Track Two Order designed EAMs predominantly for electric 
16 
utilities.  The Commission, however, has established EAMs for gas utilities, and it is 
17 
imperative that gas utilities and their shareholders continue to be rewarded similar to 
18 
electric utilities for innovative actions that run counter to their business model of earning 
19 
a rate of return on traditional plant in-service while providing benefits to customers and 
20 
advancing the CLCPA.  The Commission has recognized incentives for NPAs that avoid 
21 
traditional gas infrastructure where it is economical to do so.  However, incentives are 
22 


--- Page 62 ---
Case 23-G-0225 
Case 23-G-0226 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Customer Panel 
 
Page 60 of 60 
 
needed in other instances as well. In order to achieve the goals of the CLCPA, gas utilities 
1 
need to reduce their investment in traditional infrastructure, discourage new customers 
2 
from using natural gas, and encourage existing customers to convert from gas to electric, 
3 
all of which are counter to the traditional utility business model.  Incentives in the form of 
4 
EAMs will reward gas utilities and their shareholders for aggressively pursuing programs 
5 
that advance the CLCPA goals but result in a lower earned rate of return. 
6

Request: 
Referring to page 13, lines 4-8, of the Customer Panel's testimony, it states, "In early 2022, as 
further detailed below, National Grid engaged ESource, a research, consulting, and data science 
firm, to conduct a study to help the Companies develop a better understanding of their LMI

a.
Please provide the ESource study.
b.
How does the ESource "forward-thinking customer experience strategy" differ from the
responsibilities of the Residential Segment Energy Equity Analysts proposed by the
Companies (see page 25 of the Customer Panel testimony)?
Response: 
a.
Please see the Companies’ response to DPS-347, question 3, including DPS-347
Attachment 1 and 2, for the ESource study results.
b.
The Residential Segment Energy Equity Analyst’s responsibilities do not differ but
support the Esource “forward - thinking customer experience strategy” implementation
and adoption across KEDNY and KEDLI.  The Companies currently have half an FTE
focused on the holistic overarching strategy for the LMI segment in KEDNY and KEDLI
with no FTEs focused on the holistic strategy for Disadvantaged Communities across the
Companies’ activities.  The Residential Segment Energy Equity Analyst roles would
help align with both federal and state policy objectives related to investment in
Disadvantaged Communities and better serve LMI customers.
Energy equity recognizes that Disadvantaged Communities have been historically
marginalized and overburdened by pollution, underinvestment in clean energy
infrastructure, and lack of access to energy efficient housing and transportation.
Exhibit ___ (CP-1R) 
Page 1 of 114


--- Page 66 ---
Form 103
One analyst will be focused on developing a comprehensive energy equity strategy 
across all business units that includes the elements and recommendations from the 
Esource “forward-thinking customer experience strategy.”  This analyst will support 
the existing 0.5 FTE focused on the LMI segment strategy and follow an approach 
that combines data science and quantitative measures with ethnographic research to 
attempt to completely understand our customers and their communities.  The analyst 
will segment the residential LMI customers using Esource cohorts and the 
Companies’ current residential personas data to target programs, services, marketing, 
and communication at the microlevel.  
The second analyst will utilize Esource results and internal and external data to 
identify gaps in the current offerings while developing and growing new and existing 
programs to reach customers in Disadvantaged Communities. Additionally, the 
analyst will measure the performance of these programs and develop strategies to 
increase customer participation among LMI customers and customers in 
Disadvantaged Communities.  This work will be critical to reducing energy burdens 
and meeting the CLCPA goals of 35-40 percent of climate action benefitting 
Disadvantaged Communities.

As a supplemental response to DPS-457, please see Attachment 1 for a table that lists the 
investments included in Exhibit ___ (ITDP-4CU) in the Company’s corrections and 
updates filing in these proceedings. Additionally, Attachment 1 indicates the responses 
where the Company provided an Investment Estimation Tool (“IET”) or sanction paper 
for each discreet IT&D investment, or if not previously provided, the corresponding 
attachment included in this supplemental response where an IET or sanction paper for 
remaining investments can be found.  
As indicated in the Company’s response to DPS-457, each IET is a workbook created by 
the IT&D function to capture key attributes of proposed future investments, including the 
investment description, cost estimates, scope, timing, and the expected benefitting 
operating company, among other details. Sanction papers provide investment details that 
have gone through additional Company review and approval beyond the work performed 
in preparing an IET. For each investment where one is available, sanction papers are 
provided in lieu of the project’s IET.   
Attachments 46 and 65 – 72 contain confidential information. The Company submitted 
the confidential attachments to the Administrative Law Judges in accordance with the 
Protective Order issued in these proceedings, along with a request for confidential 
treatment.

Investment Details
Develop forward looking technology capabilities that support improved customer facing Clean Energy and Energy Efficiency service oferings:
• Continue support of functional changes in the InDemand system to meet the Energy Efficiency targets National Grid committed to with the
State Regulators.
• As the legacy out of support InDemand platform is approaching end of life and cannot be sustained for much longer National Grid is at
great risk of loosing ability to deliver Energy Efficiency programs and other energy services to our customers. The time to act is now to start
building new technology platform that provides modern and sustainable capabilities for promotion, sales, fulfillment, delivery and reporting of
Clean Energy products. New Clean Energy 2.0 platform will replace InDemand system capabilities limited to Energy Efficiency and will
include other carbon reduction and electrification programs.
• Deliver ongoing data sharing and integration with Energy Efficiency delivery partners to support existing and new clean energy
programs
• If InDemand system changes and enhancements are not implemented National Grid will not be able to achieve, track and report energy
saving targets, effectively process incentives and rebated to customers and will not earn shareholder benefit awarded by regulators
•National Grid is struggling to keep up with the fast-evolving Clean Energy (CE) needs. The current legacy InDemand application has short
comings because of the outdated platform, poor data quality and not having matured business processes in place. The Clean Energy space
is growing very rapidly and the current platform does not provide flexibility or self service capabilities to be able to react to these changes
quickly. As a result, National Grid is not meeting some existing CE objectives and incentives and is at risk for not meeting future objectives
creating significant financial and reputational risk.
•To support effective collaboration with energy efficiency programs delivery partners and to enable them to offer customer energy saving
programs National grid needs to invest in data sharing and integration solutions enabling these partners effective and compliant operations.

Benefits to Customers including:
- Saving Money: understanding home energy usage and finding ways to reduce consumption and reduce bills
- Insight: access to web portals that can analyze energy usage and provide actionable tips.
- Heating and Cooling: customized heating and cooling activities for savings and comfort
- Education: community and energy-efficiency competitions.
- Retrofits: improved decision-support tools to facilitate energy-efficiency retrofits
- Verification: measurement of energy-efficiency investments
- Real Estate: provision of energy costs for tenants and/or new home purchasers
- Solar: optimize the size and cost-effectiveness of rooftop solar panels

BC - ONLY CELLS HIGHLIGHTED GREEN SHOULD BE COMPLETED
BC & PM - ONLY CELLS HIGHLIGHTED YELLOW SHOULD BE COMPLETED
Investment Details
Green Button Connect (GBC) My Data is a means by which utilities can enable customers to share their energy consumption data with 3rd Party energy
solution providers and companies (such as Retail Energy, Solar, EE, etc..).
It allows customers to opt-in or opt-out of having their own energy usage
information sent to those third party energy efficiency providers who can then provide recommendations on energy efficiency programs. NY PSC has
mandated that NY utilities implement Green Button Connect:
- See NY Public Service Commission (PSC) mandate Case 17-E-0238 and 17-G-023 for UNY.
- See also 2022Nov11 email from Kris Kiefer Assistant General Counsel & Director (NY Regulatory) and opinion that the PSC expects gas companies to
implement GBC.
Short Term (Phase 1) of this initiative aims at extending this capability to DNY KEDNY/KEDLI monthly read customers.
Long Term (Phase 2) of this initiative is to fund analysis of a GBC redesign and implementation to resolve capacity and architecture issues related to handling
interval data which is collected for many commercial and industrial customers. This needs to integrated with the AMI solution.
Providing standardized accessibility for all NYS monthly-read customers to allow the Distributed Energy Resources (DER) market so they can make data-
informed decisions that will help NYS to achieve clean energy policy objectives.
What is the aim? What is the
Functionality?
Select 'Risk' category
from Drop Down then add
more detail.
Select 'Dependency'
category from Drop Down
then add more detail.
Select 'Assumption'
category from Drop Down
then add more detail.
Business benefits generated by this project will start in FY24 based on the assumption that the project is completed in early FY24
Project cost and duration estimate was developed in 2017 using the standard IT estimating methodology at the time.
Cost, time, and resource estimates are based on the assumption that the solution will be built similar to NIMO implementation
This investment assumes using existing API and database built with bill print data and CSS billing data
If  NG IT and/or business resources will not be available to support the project due to system wide or regional events, it will have negative impact on our customers
GBC initiative will enable secure data-sharing with the 3rd parties and will provide a simple, customer-controlled authorization process. The customer can define the length of that authorization and revoke authorization at any time. The initiative will also
ensure customer privacy, secure transmission of data, data integrity and accuracy.
Resources at UtilityAPI are available to deliver

KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
KEDNY KEDLI Case 23-G-0225/G-0226 Data Requests 
Request for Information 
FROM:
Tessie Mar
TO: 
National Grid 
SUBJECT: 
Green Button Connect 
Request: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be construed as 
requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in original electronic format 
with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
1.  
Explain whether Green Button Connect will be implemented for all KEDLI and KEDNY 
customers and customer classes.  If not, what factors did the Companies consider in 
determining which customers to target. 
2.  
Provide an estimate of the ongoing annual costs of implementing Green Button Connect 
following the initial set up costs requested for the Rate Year. 
3.  
Have the Companies leveraged any lessons learned from the implementation of Green 
Button Connect in the National Grid Upstate service territory?  If so, what information 
was leveraged and what if any affect has that had on the Green Button Connect costs 
requested by KEDLI and KEDNY? 
Response: 
1.
The Companies proposed implementation of Green Button Connect (“GBC”) would be 
available for all KEDNY and KEDLI customers and customer classes.  
2.
The Companies forecast $0.490 million in annual run-the-business costs, which consists of 
IT-related labor to maintain GBC and annual software licensing fees, as reflected in 
Attachment 1.  In addition, the Companies forecast $0.360 million in annual software 
version upgrade and refresh costs, which are also reflected in Attachment 1.  The 
Investment Estimation Tool (“IET”) provided in Attachment 1 is a workbook created by 
the Information Technology and Digital (“IT&D”) function for yet-to-be sanctioned work; 
it captures key attributes of proposed future investments.   
Exhibit ___ (CP-1R) 
Page 35 of 114


--- Page 100 ---
Form 103
Additionally, the Companies propose one-half Full-Time Equivalent (“FTE”), a Green 
Button Connect Data Analyst, to support day-to-day operations and platform optimization.  
See Diret Testimony of the Customer Panel at p. 118; see also Exhibit__ (CP-6), Schedule 
5, p. 8. 
3.
Yes, the Companies will leverage lessons learned from Niagara Mohawk Power 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid’s (“Niagara Mohawk”) GBC implementation. The 
Companies will retain those aspects of the GBC implementation that have been successful, 
while working with Niagara Mohawk to identify opportunities to improve the platform 
and make data more easily accessible to customers and third parties.  Importantly, Niagara 
Mohawk has already setup the technical connection for making meter data available to 
customers and third parties – the application program interface (“API”). The Companies 
will leverage Niagara Mohawk’s API dataflow and setup processes in their GBC rollout.

Investment Details
Green Button Connect (GBC) My Data is a means by which utilities can enable customers to share their energy consumption data with 3rd Party energy
solution providers and companies (such as Retail Energy, Solar, EE, etc..).
It allows customers to opt-in or opt-out of having their own energy usage
information sent to those third party energy efficiency providers who can then provide recommendations on energy efficiency programs. NY PSC has
mandated that NY utilities implement Green Button Connect:
- See NY Public Service Commission (PSC) mandate Case 17-E-0238 and 17-G-023 for UNY.
- See also 2022Nov11 email from Kris Kiefer Assistant General Counsel & Director (NY Regulatory) and opinion that the PSC expects gas companies to
implement GBC.
Short Term (Phase 1) of this initiative aims at extending this capability to DNY KEDNY/KEDLI monthly read customers.
Long Term (Phase 2) of this initiative is to fund analysis of a GBC redesign and implementation to resolve capacity and architecture issues related to
handling interval data which is collected for many commercial and industrial customers. This needs to integrated with the AMI solution.
Providing standardized accessibility for all NYS monthly-read customers to allow the Distributed Energy Resources (DER) market so they can make data-
informed decisions that will help NYS to achieve clean energy policy objectives.
What is the aim? What is
the Functionality?

Benefits to Customers including:
- Saving Money: understanding home energy usage and finding ways to reduce consumption and reduce bills
- Insight: access to web portals that can analyze energy usage and provide actionable tips.
- Heating and Cooling: customized heating and cooling activities for savings and comfort
- Education: community and energy-efficiency competitions.
- Retrofits: improved decision-support tools to facilitate energy-efficiency retrofits
- Verification: measurement of energy-efficiency investments
- Real Estate: provision of energy costs for tenants and/or new home purchasers
- Solar: optimize the size and cost-effectiveness of rooftop solar panels

KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid

KEDNY KEDLI Case 23-G-0225/0226 Data Requests 
Request for Information 
FROM:
Kate Overacker
TO: 
National Grid 
SUBJECT:
Green Button Connect  Program 
Request: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be construed as 
requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in original electronic format 
with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
Referring to pp. 107 - 108 of the Customer Service Panel's initial testimony, the panel states that 
the Companies propose to implement the Green Button Connect interface for the Companies' gas 
customers and state that an identical program is currently deployed to Niagara Mohawk 
customers. 
1.  
Provide any analysis, supporting documentation, workpapers, and research reports 
supporting the extension of Green Button Connect to the Companies' gas customers. 
2.  
Provide data from Niagara Mohawk regarding customer usage of Green Button Connect 
including usage reports and any customer feedback. 
Response: 
1.
Initially efforts to implement Green Button Connect My Data (“Green Button 
Connect”) focused on combination electric and gas utilities, as reflected in the Public 
Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) “Order Adopting Accelerated Energy 
Efficiency Targets” in Case 18-M-0084 (issued and effective December 13, 2018), the 
Joint Utilities’ Status Report on Green Button Connect in Cases 18-M-0084 and 98-M-
1343 (filed October 16, 2019), and the electric Distributed System Implementation 
Plans (“DSIPs”) filed in Cases 14-M-0101 and 16-M-0411.  As combination utilities 
implemented Green Button Connect, including the Companies’ Upstate New York 
affiliate Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“Niagara Mohawk”), 
Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”) encouraged the Companies to adopt the 
platform for their gas-only customers, noting that the Commission’s prior orders do not 
limit implementation to combination utilities.  With adoption of the Green Button 
Exhibit ___ (CP-1R) 
Page 51 of 114


--- Page 116 ---
Form 103
Connect functionality, customers will be able to share their energy consumption data 
with authorized third parties.  The ability to share such data is expected to provide 
valuable insights and animate the market for innovative energy-saving solutions that 
can benefit customers in line with the State’s ambitious clean energy goals. 
2.
Niagara Mohawk customers are able to share data with 43 third parties who have 
registered as part of the Company’s Green Button Connect program.  Currently, 39 
Niagara Mohawk customers are sharing data using this functionality.  Based on 
feedback from third parties, Niagara Mohawk has improved the registration process 
enabling completion of the required Data Security Agreement and Self-Attestation 
forms online.  Niagara Mohawk is also working to expand Green Button Connect 
capabilities to certain large commercial customers who were not part of the company’s 
planned advanced metering infrastructure deployment program.

Customer Service Metrics 
Request: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be construed 
as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in original electronic 
format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
1. 
For each of the metrics listed below, provide a detailed description of how the monthly 
and annual totals are calculated. Include a description of and rationale for any exceptions 
or exclusions of data. 
a. 
Public Service Commission Complaint Rate; 
b.

c.

d.

monthly and annual totals are calculated for KEDNY and KEDLI’s Public Service 
Commission Complaint Rate, Residential Customer Transaction Satisfaction, Percentage 
of Customer Calls Answered in 30 Seconds, and Adjusted Customer Bills service quality 
metrics.  The procedure also lists the data excluded from the metrics.

1. Purpose
This document describes the procedure for calculating and reporting Niagara Mohawk Power
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“NMPC”), The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National
Grid NY (“KEDNY”), and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid’s (“KEDLI”)
•
Annual customer Service Quality (“SQ”) metrics; and
•
Monthly Performance Indicator (“PI”) metrics.
Included for each metric are its: definition, data inputs, calculation, exclusions (if applicable), 
process, responsible department, reporting process, and independent review process.   
While some of the SQ and PI metrics overlap, it is important to note that the calculation of the 
metrics may differ, as described below.  The SQ metrics were established as part of rate 
plans and contain negative revenue adjustments (“NRAs”) for failing to meet established 
targets.  The calculation of these metrics is governed by the historic methodology used to 
establish the target for the metrics.  In contrast, the monthly PI metrics do not contain negative 
revenue adjustments.  For calendar years 2022-2024, NMPC’s annual reports will reflect the 
migration from the prior SQ metrics to new customer service PI titles, metrics and NRAs.  The 
PI metrics are calculated pursuant to the Commission’s Order Adopting Revisions to Customer 
Service Reporting Metrics issued and effective August 4, 2017 in Case 15-M-0566 (the “PI 
Order”) (see pages 5-7 of the PI Order) (stating that, as a product of utility rate cases, SQ 
metrics are governed by the definitions adopted in utility rate plans).  
Auditable data must be retained for the current calendar year and the prior two calendar years 
per pages 7-8 of the PI Order.

It is recognized that COVID-19 pandemic could impact the Companies’ performance under 
certain metrics.  To that end, the Companies’ current rate plans allow the Companies to petition 
the Commission to seek relief from a negative revenue adjustment to the extent it can 
demonstrate performance impacts attributable to the COVID-19 pandemic.  If the Commission 
takes action regarding service quality metrics in the Generic COVID Case, Case 20-M-0266 – 
Proceeding on Motion of the Commission Regarding the Effects of COVID-19 on Utility 
Service, the Commission’s determination in that proceeding shall control. 
2. Source of SQ and PI Metrics
2.1. NMPC SQ Metrics
a. Cases 20-E-0380 and 20-G-0381– Appendix 15 of the Joint Proposal adopted by the
Commission in its Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric
and Gas Rate Plans (issued and effective January 20, 2022) (“NMPC JP”) (see
Appendix 1 to this document).
2.2. KEDNY SQ Metrics 
a. Case 19-G-0309– Section IV.9 of the Joint Proposal (at pgs.101-106) adopted by the
Commission in its Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Gas Rate
Plans (issued and effective August 12, 2022) (“KEDNY/KEDLI JP”) (see Appendix 3
to this document).
2.3. KEDLI SQ Metrics 
a. Case 19-G-0310 – Section IV.9 of the Joint Proposal (at pgs. 101-106) adopted by the
Commission in its Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Gas Rate
Plans (issued and effective August 12, 2022) (“KEDNY/KEDLI JP”) (see Appendix 2
to this document).
2.4. Customer Service PI Metrics 
a. Case 15-M-0566 – The PI Order (see Appendix 5 to this document).

3.1. There are four SQ metrics for NMPC: 
a. PSC Complaint Rate
b. Residential Customer Transaction Satisfaction Survey
c. Small to Medium C&I Customer Transaction Satisfaction Survey
d. Percentage of Calls Answered by a Representative Within 30 Seconds
3.2. Missed Appointments Data for NMPC 
4. NMPC – PSC Complaint Rate
4.1. Definition
a. The PSC Complaint Rate is the annual average monthly rate of complaints per
100,000 customers.
b. Per the NMPC JP, a complaint is initiated with a dispute being filed by, or on the
behalf of, a consumer with the Staff of the DPS Office of Consumer Services.  The
issue of concern forming the basis for the complaint must be one within the utility’s
responsibility and control, including an action, practice, or conduct of the utility or its
employees.  Matters within the responsibility or control of alternative service
provider(s) are not counted.
4.2. Data Inputs 
a. There are two inputs: (i) the number of complaints and (ii) the customer total.
Complaints 
b. Only “charged” complaints are included in the metric.
c. A complaint by a customer to the PSC is initially registered by the PSC in the Quick
Resolution System (“QRS”).  A QRS complaint is not included in the SQ metric.  If
NMPC cannot resolve a QRS complaint with a customer, it is escalated to a “charged”
complaint in the Standard Resolution System (“SRS”).  At this stage, the complaint is
included in the metric.
d. A complaint will be considered “charged” when a customer, after first having
contacted NMPC to express dissatisfaction with an action, practice, or conduct of
Exhibit ___ (CP-1R)

NMPC, and having provided NMPC a reasonable opportunity to address the matter, 
contacts the Commission to express dissatisfaction with such action, practice, or 
conduct.  A complaint will not be charged if the contact with the Commission is for an 
opinion or inquiry.   
e. The Company enters all SRS cases into its Salesforce C3M system and runs internal
reports weekly.
f.
DPS Staff enters all SRS cases into the PSC Portal.
Customer Total 
g. The total number of customers should be the unduplicated number of customer
accounts as of December 31st of the previous calendar year.
h. A customer receiving both electric and gas service is considered one customer.
i.
The number of customers shall correspond to the number of accounts (i.e., a person
or entity with two or more accounts shall be counted as two or more customers).
j.
A customer is defined as a single active account recorded in the billing system
regardless of the number of meters.
k. Both residential and non-residential end-use customer accounts are included in the
calculation.
l.
A customer directly metered and billed on a utility’s redistribution or submetered
service class is counted as a single customer.  Individual sub meters and master
metered occupants provided service through a direct metered utility customer are not
counted separately or included in the utility’s customer total.

4.4. Exclusions

4.5. Process 
 
a. A customer calls the PSC with a complaint.   
 
b. The PSC initiates a complaint record in the PSC Portal and emails the complaint to 
the Company. 
 
c. The Lead Supervisor, Office of the President, reviews the complaint, uploads the 
complaint into the Salesforce C3M System, and assigns it to a representative to 
handle.   
 
d. A Customer Represenative or Lead Supervisor, Office of the President handles the 
complaint to resolution with the customer, working with other Company organizations, 
as appropriate.  This individual records progress in the Salesforce C3M System and 
submits the results to the PSC. 
 
e. The PSC reviews the response from the Company and closes the complaint case, if 
satisfied.

5. NMPC – Residential Customer Satisfaction Survey
5.1. Definition
a. A measure of the quality of Company services and customer relations.
5.2. Data Inputs

a. The survey populations include all residential customers of the Company in that
month that contacted the Call Center and had any one of the following transactions
with the Company: Connect, Disconnect, Electric Service Orders/Gas Service Orders,
Service Orders, Budget, High Bill, Collections, or Direct Debit.
b. NMPC will seek to perform a minimum of 600 surveys each month.
5.3. How Calculated 
a. The Residential Transaction Satisfaction Index is calculated from monthly telephone
survey results of a sample of residential customer transactions with the Company
occurring in each month of the calendar year.
b. Question No. 28 - “Overall, on a scale from 1 to 10, where 1 means dissatisfied and
10 means satisfied, how satisfied are you with the service provided by National Grid?”
is used to measure the Company’s performance under this measure.  Satisfaction is
measured by a score of 8 or higher on the question.

in the calculation of the score.
b. Customers on the “do not survey list” are excluded.

5.5. Process 
a. Each month, Analyst, Billing Operations, oversees an automated process that
involves the download of records for all survey-qualified customers from the customer
system, after which the file is sent to the Market Intelligence teams, who then
transmits them in electronic format to the Company’s survey vendor.
b. The survey vendor processes the files and then uploads the customer data files to its
Computer-Aided Telephone Interviewing (“CAITI”) system.
c. The survey vendor randomly draws phone numbers from the file and conducts the
telephone surveys.
d. The survey vendor consolidates the survey data and sends it back to the Market
Intelligence team.
e. The survey vendor loads the survey response data file into its Statistical Package for
the Social Sciences (“SPSS”), tabulates the results, and transmits the data file, PPT
file, and Excel file containing the scores to the Market Intelligence team.
f.
The Market Intelligence team conducts data quality checks by running their own
tabulations on the SPSS file and compares the results against the tabulated (PPT)
data sent by the survey vendor. The output of the data quality checks (and
corresponding raw regulatory question data) are saved monthly in an excel document
and saved on the CSPI Sharepoint Site for Backup Data.
g. The Market Intelligence team then publishes the score each month to Revenue
Requirements for insertion in the annual SQ reports.
5.6. Department Responsible 
a. Market Intelligence
